The Great Simplification · Nate Hagens 1 episode(s) · 55 insights

A New Psychology for a World in Crisis | RR 27100 min55 insights2026-08-12
▶ Watch on YouTube
IFS founder Richard 'Dick' Schwartz and climate psychologist Stephie Bednarek apply the Internal Family Systems map of exiles, managers, firefighters and Self to the global economic superorganism, arguing that climate defenses have escalated from denial to disavowal toward outright loss of reality and that pushing more information now generates backlash rather than change. Bednarek's structural counter-cases (Sweden's paternity leave going from ~6% to ~96% only after a legal change; ~2.1% of global financial wealth reaching climate initiatives; a 25% social tipping point) frame inner work as necessary but insufficient, while Schwartz details IFS's push into leader training, a public workbook and a non-therapist AI product across a ~50,000-practitioner base.
IFS Part Architecture as Operating Model · 7Denial to Disavowal to Reality Loss · 10Putting the Superorganism on the Couch · 8Capital, Elites and Dark Triad Selection · 7Structure Beats Mindset · 7Scaling IFS: Distribution, Product and AI · 5Activist Burnout and Neurodivergence · 5Soul, Spirit and the Simplification Endgame · 6
IFS Part Architecture as Operating Model 7
Internal Family Systems models the mind as a system of sub-personalities in three functional roles — exiles (parts frozen by trauma that carry 'burdens' of extreme belief and emotion), managers (pre-emptive protectors that run your life so the wound never repeats), and firefighters (impulsive parts that douse an exile once it erupts) — plus an undamageable 'Self'.
framework🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: highInternal Family SystemsIFSpsychotherapytrauma therapy
So what: A four-slot taxonomy that can be mapped onto individuals, organizations, markets and states — useful as a reusable diagnostic for behavior that looks irrational.
Burdens are described as behaving like a virus injected into otherwise healthy parts, meaning the part itself is never the pathology — the extreme belief or emotion it carries is, which is why IFS treats no part as bad and refuses to eliminate any of them.
framework🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: highInternal Family SystemsIFS
So what: Reframes 'bad actors' — internal or organizational — as protective functions that can be renegotiated rather than removed; a different intervention logic than purge-and-replace.
“there are no bad parts of you” ▶ 1:34:00
IFS's evolutionary justification is the modular-mind hypothesis: a single undivided mind cannot attend to enough survival-relevant problems simultaneously, so a collection of specialized sub-minds that dialogue with each other — ideally under one leader — is adaptive.
causal🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: mediummodular mind hypothesisevolutionary psychologyIFS
So what: Gives the model an evolutionary rather than merely clinical grounding, which is what lets it be extended to collective systems later in the conversation.
Self is explicitly not a passive mindfulness witness state: Schwartz insists it carries clarity, courage and confidence, sees injustice without denial, and acts — which is why he trains activists to act from Self rather than from righteous parts.
thesis🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: highIFSSelfsocial activismmindfulness
So what: Counters the common critique that inner work is quietist; positions IFS as compatible with, not a substitute for, external structural action.
Self-leadership is a spectrum, not a binary — Schwartz's benchmark is Hagens estimating he is genuinely in Self roughly 10-15% of his life, and Schwartz confirming the percentage rises with healing but never hits 100%, since his wife can still trigger him.
data_point🗣 Nate Hagens, hostconviction: mediumIFSSelf
So what: Sets a realistic base rate: any thesis that depends on leaders operating 'from Self' must assume small, improvable percentages rather than transformed individuals.
“But that's probably 10 or 15% of my life.” ▶ 1:09:52
When a client cannot locate or feel any parts, IFS treats that inability itself as a protective part — usually distrust of the therapist's judgment — rather than as evidence the model fails for that person, which makes the framework structurally unfalsifiable from the inside.
framework🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: mediumIFSpsychotherapy
So what: Worth flagging for anyone evaluating IFS as an evidence-based product: the model absorbs its own negative cases.
Schwartz claims IFS's 40-plus-year-old map has held up unchanged, and positions it as practical rather than esoteric compared with integral theory or ego-development theory — the whole protocol is locate the feeling in the body, move the disliking parts back, get curious, ask, and wait for the answer.
thesis🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: highIFSintegral theoryego development theoryattachment theory
So what: Low training overhead is the actual distribution advantage over rival developmental frameworks; simplicity is the moat.
Denial to Disavowal to Reality Loss 10
Bednarek argues climate defenses have escalated in a specific sequence: long-standing outright denial shifted over roughly the last decade into 'disavowal' — holding two contradictory realities simultaneously — because climate data became too available to deny outright.
framework🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highclimate psychologyclimate changedenialdisavowal
So what: Predicts that better information campaigns no longer move the marginal person; the binding constraint has moved from data availability to psychological digestion capacity.
“disavow is where two realities exist at the same time” ▶ 26:12
The canonical disavowal case Bednarek gives is a CEO who genuinely loves his children and the natural world at home, then crosses into a work role where that is split off and he enacts decisions he knows rob those children of a safe future — with both realities running in parallel without collision.
causal🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highdisavowalcorporate leadershipclimate change
So what: Explains why individual values screening of executives predicts nothing about corporate behavior — role, not belief, is the operative variable.
“And then something happens on the way from home to work where they slip into a role where a lot of that is split off and they enact something that they know robs their children of a safe future.” ▶ 27:10
Bednarek predicts a third stage beyond denial and disavowal — an outright loss of reality — as external reality intensifies while external support structures for metabolizing it remain absent, forcing people to either collapse into unbearable emotion or become more extreme in their defenses.
prediction🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: mediumclimate psychologypolarizationdisavowal
So what: Frames rising political irrationality as a predictable psychological output of unmetabolized ecological information, not as an independent variable.
“which is a proper um loss of reality” ▶ 29:32
Pushing more traumatic climate information at the public is actively counterproductive because disavowal is protective: shaming or force-feeding data attacks a genuine vulnerability with no external support system behind it, so protectors respond with more aggression, bypassing and scapegoating.
contrarian🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highclimate activismclimate communicationclimate psychology
So what: Directly against the standard advocacy playbook — implies communication budgets are producing backlash rather than conversion.
Bednarek states there is a dynamic feedback relationship between the volume of climate activism and information pushed out and the severity of defensive public responses, meaning the backlash is endogenous to the campaign rather than an external headwind.
causal🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highclimate activismpolarizationclimate communication
So what: If true, activism intensity is a leading indicator of reactionary political intensity — the two should be modeled together, not separately.
Schwartz's structural explanation is that denial is a manager part whose job is to keep negative information out entirely — because letting it in would trigger exiles and turn an external problem into an internal catastrophe — which is why denial evaporates on its own once exiles are healed rather than needing to be argued away.
causal🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: highIFSdenialclimate psychology
So what: Implies the leverage point on denial is emotional capacity, not evidence — an entirely different intervention target than fact-checking.
“One of your managers is going to be denial.” ▶ 22:57
Bednarek argues Western culture actively manufactures the denial by supplying an enormous menu of firefighting options on demand, feeding hope with progressively less grounding in reality, and supplying scapegoating stories that convert fear into blame.
causal🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highclimate psychologymediaWestern culture
So what: Positions distraction supply — screens, entertainment, scapegoat narratives — as a structural climate variable rather than a consumer preference.
There is no word in the culture for earth grief, so the appropriate emotional response to ecological loss becomes what Bednarek calls a culturally exiled emotion — the distress is normal and the absence of an outlet is the pathology, not the distress itself.
thesis🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highclimate griefclimate psychologyFrancis Weller
So what: Reframes climate anxiety from a mental-health cost line into an unmet infrastructure need — rituals, containers and community formats.
“Nobody wants discomfort and distress. But that's the appropriate response to the external conditions.” ▶ 0:20
Climate psychologists are routinely hired with the implicit brief of making distress disappear so employees keep functioning inside the existing system — a demand Bednarek treats as the sector's core ethical problem rather than its product.
contrarian🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highclimate psychologycorporate wellbeingESG
So what: Corporate climate-wellbeing spending may be buying suppression, not resilience; a reputational and efficacy risk for that service category.
“the implicit invitation is often, can you make this go away?” ▶ 0:06
Bednarek compares mainstream psychotherapy's handling of emotion to agricultural monoculture — certain emotions are wanted, others are not, and 'psychological roundup' is applied to whatever causes discomfort — whereas IFS's advantage is permitting an ecology of parts where hope and hopelessness coexist.
framework🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highclimate psychologyIFSpsychotherapymonoculture
So what: A memorable diagnostic for any system that optimizes for a single emotional or behavioral output while destroying its own diversity.
“there's an ecology of parts and I can be hopeful and hopeless and these contradicting things can be there at the same time.” ▶ 0:32
Putting the Superorganism on the Couch 8
Asked what parts the global economic superorganism would present in therapy, Schwartz names the striving part that treats growth as the terminal goal and cannot consider the consequences of unrestricted growth — and says the clinical move is to ask it what it fears would happen if it stopped, which reveals the legacy burden and the vulnerable parts it is distracting from.
framework🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: higheconomic superorganismeconomic growthIFS
So what: Recasts the growth imperative as a protective behavior with a fear underneath it, implying growth-compulsion has an addressable driver rather than being a brute constant.
Scaled to the global level, Hagens and Schwartz agree the exiles of the system are entire nation-states — the wounded, ignored countries whose unmetabolized pain keeps the system's protectors from ever slowing down.
framework🗣 Nate Hagens, hostconviction: mediumeconomic superorganismgeopoliticsIFS
So what: A non-obvious mapping: global stability may depend on addressing the neglected periphery, framed here as a precondition for the core slowing its growth compulsion.
“a lot of the exiles are countries themselves” ▶ 46:45
Bednarek's first objection to treating the superorganism is that no neutral therapist exists — the clinician is as embedded in the superorganism's logic and incentives as the client, producing collusion that blinds the therapist to what is actually being presented.
thesis🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: higheconomic superorganismsystems changeclimate psychology
So what: A general warning for consultants, ESG advisors and change agents: your diagnostic instrument is made of the thing you are diagnosing.
“there is no neutral therapist” ▶ 49:33
Powerful manager parts — individual or institutional — never enter therapy to surrender power; they enter because they dislike being held to account or dislike the unintended consequences of their own behavior and want those consequences removed.
framework🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highcorporate leadershipsystems changeIFS
So what: Predicts the shape of every voluntary corporate reform effort: symptom removal, not power transfer, is the actual brief being purchased.
“manager parts that hold an enormous amount of power don't tend to come to therapy to give up that power” ▶ 49:54
Bednarek's conclusion is that the superorganism will only present for treatment at rock bottom, and since you cannot make an unwilling client change, the transition necessarily entails an enormous amount of disaster for humans and more-than-humans first.
prediction🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: higheconomic superorganismcollapseclimate change
So what: The bear case stated plainly by a practitioner: no voluntary systemic course correction before crisis — plan for crisis-forced adaptation, not pre-emptive reform.
“the super organism might potentially come to therapy only when it hits rock bottom” ▶ 50:14
Applied to organizations, IFS becomes a fractal audit of structure rather than individuals: which departments are set up to be polarized and never reconciled, which voices are habitually exiled, which data never gets an airing, and which knowledge systems are routinely dismissed.
framework🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highorganizational designsystems changeIFS
So what: A concrete due-diligence checklist for organizational risk — structurally exiled information is where the unpriced problems live.
Bednarek insists that 'everything belongs' applies even to the parts of a system trending toward fascism — they are system outputs signaling something unheard, and the practitioner's job is to engage and listen rather than excise them.
contrarian🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: mediumfascismpolarizationsystems changeIFS
So what: An explicitly anti-purge stance on political extremism that most institutional actors would reject; treats extremism as diagnostic signal.
The opening exercise in Bednarek's systems wayfinding programme is to deliberately go to 'the wrong pub' and order the wrong drink in order to sit long enough to hear the wrong people have the wrong conversation — the point being to surface the participant's own judgmental parts and identity attachments, not to persuade anyone.
anecdote🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highsystems wayfindingCenter for Climate Psychologypolarization
So what: A cheap, replicable de-polarization protocol that targets the observer rather than the target audience.
“go to the wrong pup and order the wrong drink” ▶ 53:13
Capital, Elites and Dark Triad Selection 7
Bednarek cites roughly 2.1% of global financial wealth flowing to climate initiatives, and says shaming wealth holders over that gap does not move capital — so her programme instead used commissioned artwork with artist Alan Balden to let a room of 100 wealth holders and philanthropists recognize their own parts without confessing anything.
data_point🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highphilanthropyclimate financeAlan BaldenCenter for Climate Psychology
So what: A concrete number on the philanthropic climate gap plus an empirically-tested unlocking method — relevant to anyone designing capital-mobilization campaigns.
“2.1% of um the global financial wealth goes to climate initiatives” ▶ 55:01
The image that landed hardest with wealth holders was of building a wall of money around oneself for protection — something no individual would voluntarily admit in a group, but which many affirmed once the facilitator pre-framed it as an unavoidable adaptation to living inside the Western system rather than a personal failing.
anecdote🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highphilanthropywealth managementCenter for Climate Psychology
So what: De-shaming as a technical precondition for capital movement — a repeatable design principle for family-office and philanthropic engagement.
“I want to build a wall of money around me to protect myself” ▶ 55:40
Bednarek observes a reliable part-switch in philanthropic negotiations: donors engage generously from a 'parent' part wanting a safer future for children, but as the conversation moves toward actual capital transfer, 'the part that has gone to business school' takes over and reframes it as return on investment, control and reputational risk.
causal🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highphilanthropyimpact investingclimate finance
So what: Explains the persistent gap between pledged and deployed philanthropic capital, and pinpoints the exact stage of the funnel where intent evaporates.
“the part that has gone to business school kicks back in” ▶ 57:10
Hagens states flatly that the current system does not select or reward people led by Self energy, and Schwartz concedes the point — it takes ruthlessness to reach the top, so dark-triad traits are a selection outcome of the institutional filter, not an accident.
causal🗣 Nate Hagens, hostconviction: highdark triadnarcissismpsychopathycorporate leadership
So what: Any thesis premised on enlightened leadership emerging voluntarily has to beat this selection filter first — structural change must precede leadership change.
“Except our current system doesn't select or reward people that would be led by self energy.” ▶ 1:00:21
Schwartz reframes narcissism, Machiavellianism and psychopathy as protector parts shielding large exile loads rather than fixed character — and reports that once such leaders can separate from and observe those parts instead of hating themselves, access to Self and real healing opens up.
thesis🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: mediumdark triadnarcissismpsychopathyIFScorporate leadership
So what: Suggests executive dysfunction is treatable in principle, but only after a crisis makes the executive willing — which is rare by construction.
“It takes that kind of ruthlessness often to get to the top.” ▶ 1:02:34
Schwartz reports two distinct outcomes among the CEO-type clients he has worked with: some had to quit because the company culture made staying in Self impossible, while others succeeded in changing the entire culture of their company.
anecdote🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: mediumcorporate leadershiporganizational cultureIFS
So what: A bifurcated outcome distribution — culture change from the top is possible but the modal result is executive exit, which is a talent-drain risk for incumbents.
“I've worked with some of these CEO types that wound up having to quit because they couldn't stay in that system.” ▶ 1:03:08
Schwartz's institutional bet is explicitly a trickle-down bet: he rejects economic trickle-down entirely but argues Self is contagious, so a self-led leader pulls for the Self of everyone around them — hence new IFS trainings aimed at leaders and at coaches who work with leaders.
thesis🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: highIFS Institutecorporate leadershipexecutive coaching
So what: Names the go-to-market: leadership and coach training as the distribution channel into institutions, with contagion as the claimed multiplier.
“economic trickle down theory doesn't work at all but uh I believe that self can trickle down from the leadership” ▶ 59:47
Structure Beats Mindset 7
Bednarek's counterexample to mindset-first change is Swedish paternity leave: communication campaigns and mindset-change efforts moved uptake almost not at all from roughly 6%, but a legal rule that no parent gets the money unless both take leave pushed uptake to about 96% — and only then did mindsets follow.
data_point🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highSwedenparental leavebehavior changepublic policy
So what: Hard evidence that regulatory design, not persuasion, is the high-leverage instrument — and that attitude change is often a lagging output of structural change.
“suddenly um 96% of um all dads that were in a relationship um took parental leave” ▶ 1:07:25
Bednarek warns that coaching an individual leader toward greater values-awareness inside an unchanged structure creates a double bind — all the burden of change lands on the individual, whose life gets materially harder while the superorganism around them stays fixed.
causal🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highexecutive coachingorganizational developmentsystems change
So what: A direct critique of the executive-coaching value proposition; predicts attrition and disillusionment rather than institutional change as the modal outcome.
“I create enorm an enormous amount of tension where all the change is down to me personally” ▶ 1:04:47
Bednarek identifies a self-fulfilling industry loop: leaders who become more aware typically leave the system rather than change it from within, many then become coaches, and those coaches train more coaches to do the same — recycling talent out of the institutions that most needed changing.
contrarian🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highexecutive coachingorganizational developmentsystems change
So what: A structural indictment of the coaching sector's aggregate impact; the industry may be a talent sink dressed as a change lever.
“So it becomes a sort of um self-fulfilling industry.” ▶ 1:05:51
Hagens draws out the labor-market consequence: people who metabolize the predicament and leave corporate roles find coaching is nearly the only vocation that aligns with their new orientation, so there is a large unmet need for jobs and vocations for people who have come to terms with the situation — and Bednarek adds those jobs are badly paid.
thesis🗣 Nate Hagens, hostconviction: mediumlabor marketexecutive coachingcareer transition
So what: Points at a structural vocational gap and an income cliff — a real constraint on how fast values-driven talent can exit incumbent industries.
Bednarek argues the practitioner's core competence is diagnostic altitude — enough systems sensing to know when to intervene at the individual level versus when only structural intervention will work — and that inner-change and systemic-change practitioners currently operate in fragmented silos.
framework🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highsystems changeclimate psychologyorganizational development
So what: Names the actual scarce skill and the market gap between inner-work and structural-change disciplines.
Bednarek invokes the social tipping point literature to argue only about 25% of a culture — not 100% — needs to reach this wider, polarity-holding state of being for the tipping point to occur, and that the surrounding context must supply feedback loops that reward the stretch.
data_point🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highsocial tipping pointssystems changepolicy
So what: A quantified threshold makes the thesis testable and makes targeted elite/policymaker adoption a plausible strategy rather than mass conversion.
“we don't need 100% of a culture to do that. We need 25% of a culture in order to have a social tipping point.” ▶ 1:32:03
Bednarek argues self-regulation is itself structurally dependent — she can access Self far more easily when other people hold her in it — so the intervention target is communal rites and rituals oriented to relationship and belonging rather than accumulation, busyness and striving, echoing Francis Weller's earlier appearance on the show.
thesis🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highFrancis Wellercommunityritualclimate grief
So what: Moves the unit of intervention from the individual to the container, implying demand for community infrastructure rather than individual therapy products.
Scaling IFS: Distribution, Product and AI 5
Schwartz estimates roughly 50,000 people globally are trained in IFS, each working with additional clients — the installed practitioner base underpinning any claim about the model's reach.
data_point🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: mediumIFS InstituteIFSpsychotherapy
So what: A concrete market-size anchor for the modality and a base for estimating downstream reach through practitioner leverage.
“Well, just an estimate, but my best estimate is about 50,000.” ▶ 15:31
Schwartz says he deliberately withheld IFS from direct public use for years because inviting people to approach exiled parts unsupervised produced big backlash reactions — and only after solving for safe self-delivery did the institute begin releasing a workbook and public teaching events.
anecdote🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: highIFS Instituteself-helppsychotherapy
So what: Explains the delayed consumerization of IFS and flags the specific safety risk any competitor productizing parts-work will hit.
The IFS Institute is in active discussions on an AI product explicitly positioned as not an AI therapist — instead a tool to help people get to know their parts and work between sessions — differentiating from the existing crop of AI therapy apps.
prediction🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: mediumIFS Instituteartificial intelligenceAI therapydigital health
So what: A named incumbent modality moving into AI-assisted between-session support; a competitive and regulatory-positioning signal for the mental-health AI category.
“we're now talking to people about some kind of AI product, not an AI therapist” ▶ 33:33
Schwartz states his explicit goal is for IFS to replace the prevailing paradigm for understanding and relating to the mind, conceding it likely will not happen in his lifetime, and framing anything short of that paradigm shift as merely Bateson's first-order change.
thesis🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: highIFSGregory Batesonparadigm change
So what: Names the terminal ambition and the theory of change — paradigm replacement, not incremental adoption — which sets the yardstick for judging the institute's moves.
“for IFS to replace the current paradigm for understanding and relating to to yourself” ▶ 1:28:59
Schwartz is writing a book on the 'guides' phenomenon — entities that show up in self-led clients and explicitly answer that they are neither a part nor Self — a spiritual turn he only became publicly open about this year after 30 years of privately observing it.
anecdote🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: mediumIFSspiritualityCarl Jung
So what: A forthcoming publication that will move IFS further from clinical positioning toward the spiritual, with reputational upside and downside for institutional adoption.
“it's a whole phenomena that I'm actually writing a book on” ▶ 1:24:28
Activist Burnout and Neurodivergence 5
Schwartz's mechanism for activist burnout is specific: activists carry a part that absorbs the suffering of the world and believes it must feel that suffering constantly in order to help — and once that part learns the activism does not require it, burnout resolves without loss of motivation.
causal🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: highactivismburnoutIFS
So what: A testable intervention on a chronic failure mode in advocacy organizations — retention risk that is currently mispriced as workload.
“many of those activists have a part that takes in the pain of the world and thinks to help people it has to do that” ▶ 40:30
Activists commonly fear that healing the wound driving them will remove their motivation to act; Schwartz says that turns out not to be true at all, and that self-led work produces a wellspring of energy that does not tire in the same way.
contrarian🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: highactivismburnoutIFS
So what: Removes the main objection activists raise to doing inner work — the fear of losing their edge.
Bednarek's Center for Climate Psychology ran a series on neurodivergence in the metacrisis, arguing neurodivergence is a superpower rather than a pathology in this domain because a highly aware, systemically-sensing nervous system makes more connections and finds unusual leverage points.
thesis🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highneurodivergenceCenter for Climate Psychologymetacrisis
So what: A concrete hiring and retention thesis: treat neurodivergent staff as the systemic-sensing asset instead of funneling them back to the mainstream paradigm through HR initiatives.
“a lot of people who are neurodeivergent are in the climate space” ▶ 43:27
Bednarek uses the Medusa myth as an operating rule for anyone working on the predicament — stare at the abyss too long and you turn to stone — arguing the subject is too big for one skin to bear and must be approached communally and sometimes sideways through play.
framework🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highmythologyburnoutclimate psychology
So what: A practical dosing heuristic for sustained work on catastrophic subject matter; supports community and levity as functional requirements, not indulgences.
“if you stare at the abyss for too long you risk turning to stone” ▶ 37:05
Bednarek names as a widespread cultural exile the wild, undomesticated capacity to feel love and grief — to fall to one's knees and howl together — as opposed to the polite tears wiped away in a therapist's office, and says accessing Self through community rather than introspection widens capacity without removing awareness of what is coming.
thesis🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highclimate griefcommunityritualIFS
So what: Positions collective expressive practice, not private clinical treatment, as the missing capacity-building technology.
“there's this deep grief that comes in” ▶ 38:48
Soul, Spirit and the Simplification Endgame 6
Schwartz's current position is that Self is the particle state of a larger wave he calls the oceanic self — the same place reached via psychedelics or deep meditation — which is why it cannot be damaged and shows up identically in everyone.
thesis🗣 Richard 'Dick' Schwartz, IFS founderconviction: highIFSpsychedelicsmeditationnon-duality
So what: Places IFS in the same access category as psychedelics without the substance — a competitive framing versus the psychedelic-therapy sector.
“It's the place you go with psychedelics. It's the place you go when you meditate a lot.” ▶ 1:16:31
Bednarek's use of 'soul' is Jungian and relational rather than ascendant — a mercurial quality living between relations that binds us into life through participation, communicates via dreams, body sensation and the imaginal, disappears if grasped intellectually, and is explicitly not interested in happiness.
framework🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highCarl JungFrancis Wellermythologysoul
So what: A different value function from wellbeing optimization: meaning through participation and staying with the trouble, which reframes what 'quality of life' metrics should even track.
In systems wayfinding pilots Bednarek experimentally asked whether soul is a part, and reports participants' answer came back that it is neither a part nor Self but something larger — which then spontaneously took people on undirected embodied journeys of interconnection resembling shamanic or psychedelic experience without any substance.
anecdote🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: mediumsystems wayfindingIFSpsychedelicsThich Nhat Hanh
So what: If reproducible, a zero-substance route to psychedelic-class experiences — directly relevant to the economics of the psychedelic therapy sector.
“I'm not a part. I'm also not self.” ▶ 1:20:22
Hagens frames the show's core premise as fixed: human behavior is emergent from energy and the economy, and the great simplification following the fossil-fuel peak is inevitable either way — so the only open variable is whether the descent is brutal or humane.
thesis🗣 Nate Hagens, hostconviction: highgreat simplificationfossil fuelsenergyeconomic superorganism
So what: The host's stated priors define what any intervention is being evaluated against: not avoidance of contraction, but modulation of its violence.
“And so the real question is whether that descent is brutal or humane.” ▶ 1:24:53
Bednarek concedes directly that no psychological approach can stop the biodiversity loss already underway, repositioning the entire field's value proposition as building collective containers for meeting loss rather than preventing it — with hospice work as the explicit analogue.
thesis🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: highbiodiversity lossclimate psychologyhospice
So what: An unusually candid framing from inside the sector: the deliverable is capacity to metabolize loss, which is a fundamentally different service than mitigation.
“no psychological approach can stop the unfolding of um biodiversity loss that is already underway” ▶ 1:25:44
Bednarek's closing intervention, if granted a magic wand, is to have decision makers physically stand in the places they decide about — citing artist Tracy Wall asking water policy makers to step into the polluted rivers as they took decisions.
framework🗣 Stephie Bednarek, climate psychologistconviction: mediumTracy Wallpublic policyenvironmental governance
So what: A cheap, concrete governance design idea: embodied proximity as a corrective to abstracted decision making.
“step into water as they made the decisions into the polluted rivers” ▶ 1:36:22

All-In Podcast 1 episode(s) · 54 insights

Rahm Emanuel: Trump's Foreign Policy, China, Europe's Decline, Immigration & DSA vs Democrats72 min54 insights2026-08-13
▶ Watch on YouTube
Rahm Emanuel sketches a 2028-shaped platform on All-In: replace unilateral tariffs with an allied economic bloc to 'isolate the isolator' against a China he says exports its own economic dysfunction, close a live Indo-Pacific deterrence gap centered on the Philippines and Japan rather than a 2027 Taiwan date, and rebuild US competitiveness via the bipartisan Dignity Act, a third-grade-first education and skilled-trades overhaul, and revenue-side fiscal repair including step-up-basis reform and a 10% levy on prediction markets and sports betting funding NIH, NSF and DARPA. He clashes on air with David Friedberg over whether Europe's decline is a leftist failure (Emanuel: Merkel was CDU) and whether federal subsidy causes cost inflation, and formally reads the DSA out of the Democratic Party.
China's Dysfunction Export & the Allied Economic Bloc · 8Indo-Pacific Deterrence Gap: Taiwan, Philippines, Japan · 9Middle East Realignment After the Iran War · 5Europe's Self-Inflicted Decline · 6Immigration as Talent Strategy · 5Education-to-Workforce Overhaul · 7Fiscal Reckoning, Cost Disease & the Tax Code · 9Democratic Party vs. the DSA · 5
China's Dysfunction Export & the Allied Economic Bloc 8
Rahm Emanuel argues China's largest export is not autos or electronics but its own domestic economic dysfunction, dumped on the world as overcapacity that is crushing other countries' industrial bases — with more WTO cases filed against China than against all other countries combined.
framework🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highChinaXiWTOChileIndonesiaBrazilautossteel
So what: Frames Chinese export volume as distress-dumping rather than competitiveness — expect more anti-dumping actions and tariff walls in EM industrial economies, a persistent margin headwind for global steel, autos and textiles.
“Xi is largest export is not autos electronics. It's the domestic dis economic dysfunction of China.” ▶ 5:46
China's auto industry is exporting more cars than ever while industry profits are down 20%, which Emanuel cites as evidence the export push is loss-making capacity dumping rather than genuine competitiveness.
data_point🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highChinaauto industryEV exports
So what: Volume-up/profit-down is the signature of subsidized overcapacity; watch Chinese OEM margins and European/EM tariff responses as the pressure valve.
“their auto industry is now exporting more cars and their profits are down 20%” ▶ 6:34
Emanuel's core China prescription is to replace unilateral tariffs with a formal allied economic bloc spanning Australia, Japan, South Korea, Taiwan, the EU and Latin America, using the bloc's time and scale to rebuild atrophied US capacity — a doctrine he sums up as 'isolate the isolator.'
thesis🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highAustraliaJapanSouth KoreaTaiwanEUBrazilLatin AmericaChina
So what: A 2028 Democratic administration under this doctrine would be bloc-building rather than tariff-first — supportive of allied trade corridors and cross-border supply-chain capex, bearish for pure bilateral tariff plays.
“rather than have tariffs we should have an economic block so all of us together can withstand China” ▶ 7:27
Emanuel lays out a four-tool national security toolbox — military power, political persuasion, economic statecraft, and cultural attraction — and classifies US universities, national labs, research and technology as economic statecraft, arguing the current administration is degrading three of the four.
framework🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highUnited Statesnational labsuniversitiesDARPAresearch funding
So what: Ties US science-funding budget lines directly to national security valuation — implies research funding is politically defensible and eventually restorable, a floor under the sector.
“One is military power, second is political persuasion, third is economic statecraftraft, and fourth is cultural attraction.” ▶ 8:25
Emanuel says the 'win-win' framing of Xi's China was always false — Beijing's doctrine is to make counterparties dependent on China while China stays independent of them, deliberately maintaining duplicate suppliers such as Brazilian and Argentine soybeans against US soybeans, and Australian barley alternatives.
causal🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highChinaXisoybeansBrazilArgentinabarleyAustralia
So what: Explains why US agricultural export leverage keeps failing — supplier redundancy is deliberate Beijing policy, so US ag exposure to China should be priced as structurally impaired.
“China wants everybody dependent on China and them independent of you that is their theory of the case.” ▶ 12:56
Emanuel uses COVID vaccines as the template for how China learns and closes gaps: the US produced a 90%+ efficacy vaccine in roughly 12 months versus China's 18-19 months at around 60% efficacy, after which Xi made a massive life-sciences investment that put China in near-parity pursuit of the US within five years.
data_point🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highChinaXiPfizerModernaOperation Warp Speedlife sciences
So what: Sets a five-year clock on Chinese biotech parity — a direct pricing and margin risk for US/EU pharma and a reason to expect accelerating China-originated licensing deals.
“within 12 months 14 whatever United States develops a vaccine that has a 90 plus% efficacy. China takes 18 19 months and their efficacy is in the 60%” ▶ 14:02
Emanuel says every Trump budget has proposed a 40% NIH cut with Republicans settling near 15%, and calls it unilateral disarmament against a China compounding its life-sciences investment — citing an NIH-funded pancreatic drug now showing promise against three or four other cancers.
data_point🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highNIHNational Science FoundationDARPATrump administrationChinapancreatic cancer
So what: Sustained NIH cuts starve the early-stage pipeline US biotech monetizes 10-15 years out — a slow-burn negative for domestic drug discovery and a relative tailwind for ex-US originators.
“Every budget he's proposed a 40% cut in NIH. Every budget.” ▶ 15:05
David Friedberg argues China is now attacking the knowledge-and-services base the US economy has depended on for 30 years, layering open-sourced AI on top of a path to ~8 terawatts of electricity production versus the US struggling to reach 2 by 2040, plus 20x the US manufacturing square footage, much of it automated.
thesis🗣 David Friedbergconviction: highChinaAIopen source AIelectricitymanufacturingpharmaceuticals
So what: If Chinese open-weight models commoditize services and IP the way Chinese factories commoditized goods, US services-sector pricing power — the market's largest margin pool — is the next thing to reprice.
“now we're seeing them effectively eliminate the capacity to compete in knowledge, services, and IP” ▶ 11:49
Indo-Pacific Deterrence Gap: Taiwan, Philippines, Japan 9
Emanuel says the debate over whether China moves on Taiwan in 2027 or 2029 is a distraction because escalation is already underway — intensified confrontation with the Philippines, more aggressive activity around the Senkaku Islands, and a live missile fired from a submarine near Australia.
contrarian🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highTaiwanChinaPhilippinesJapanSenkaku IslandsAustraliaVietnam
So what: Reprices Indo-Pacific risk from a dated 2027 event-risk model to continuous, already-live escalation — relevant to defense budgets, shipping insurance and regional risk premia today rather than in two years.
“So, you say 2027. I'm like I'm looking at today and say you don't get you don't get to wait. It's happening now.” ▶ 23:55
Emanuel draws a sharp line inside strategic ambiguity: the political question of one-China is deliberately ambiguous, but US opposition to changing the status quo through kinetic force is not ambiguous at all.
framework🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highTaiwanChinaTaiwan Relations ActUnited States
So what: Clarifies the actual red line a future administration would defend — useful for separating political provocations from physical ones when Taiwan headlines hit.
“We keep the politics ambiguous, but as it relates to using kinetic force to change the status quo, we're not ambiguous.” ▶ 21:07
Emanuel identifies three distinct Chinese options against Taiwan — an economic quarantine that suffocates the island, a 96-hour invasion, and an incremental seizure of the three small Taiwan-associated islands off the mainland coast — and notes China has historically executed all three against different countries.
framework🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highTaiwanChinablockadequarantineislands
So what: The incremental-islands scenario is the most likely and least-priced: it delivers a Taiwan risk shock without a war, and markets typically model only full invasion.
“One is the economic quarantine that just suffocates Taiwan. One is an invasion that tries to end this in 96 hours.” ▶ 25:21
Emanuel argues the flashpoint likely to come before Taiwan is the South China Sea, where the Philippines — unlike Taiwan — is a sovereign country, a US treaty ally, and the winner of the 2016 arbitration case China ignored, and where he says US troops and resources should be doubled to back President Marcos.
prediction🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highPhilippinesSouth China SeaScarborough ShoalChinaMarcos2016 arbitration
So what: An underwatched escalation vector carrying a live US treaty obligation — a Philippines incident could trigger US involvement faster than a Taiwan one.
“And Philippines is not Taiwan. It's a country. It's a treaty ally. And it won a 2016 case.” ▶ 24:26
Emanuel makes a causal argument that US bogdown in the Strait of Hormuz over the Iran war directly enabled Chinese escalation in the Indo-Pacific, because a White House consumed by one crisis becomes a one-ball juggler and loses focus on the theater that matters more.
causal🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highIranStrait of HormuzChinaIndo-PacificWhite House
So what: A tradeable coupling: Middle East escalation should be read as a leading indicator for Indo-Pacific risk, not as an unrelated theater.
“when there's a for the White House becomes a one ball juggler” ▶ 23:14
Emanuel calls Japan the long pole of US Indo-Pacific policy, citing Okinawa as America's largest single-country military footprint, the only permanently forward-based aircraft carrier in the world, and five consecutive years of Japan being the number one foreign direct investor in the US and vice versa.
data_point🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highJapanOkinawaUnited StatesFDIQuadSouth Korea
So what: Quantifies why Japan is the least-substitutable US alliance — Japanese equities and US-Japan capital flows carry embedded strategic support.
“Look Japan is the long pole of America's Indopacific policy.” ▶ 26:54
Emanuel says Japan's prime minister publicly stated what the US spent 30 years trying to get a Japanese leader to say — that a Chinese move on Taiwan would be a Japanese national security threat — and China responded with simultaneous economic coercion, political coercion and military intimidation.
data_point🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highJapanTaiwanChinaJapanese prime minister
So what: Japan is now an explicit Chinese coercion target; watch Chinese import bans, tourism restrictions and Senkaku activity as the instruments pressuring Japanese sectors.
Emanuel supports the administration's yen intervention but attributes it largely to US self-interest in defending the Treasury market, given the pressure the US debt load is putting on Treasuries.
causal🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: mediumyenUS TreasuriesJapanUS debtcurrency intervention
So what: Explicitly links FX intervention policy to Treasury-market fragility — implies further coordinated dollar/yen management if long-end yields back up.
“I think there's a lot of self-interest as it relates to our treasuries and our dollars and the pressure on our treasuries right now because of the amount of debt we have” ▶ 28:28
Emanuel argues the US over-optimized for the war on terror and missed a battlefield technology transformation, evidenced by Iran and Ukraine both controlling waterways near their fronts without possessing navies, and says US deterrence is very weakened right now.
framework🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highIranUkrainedronesUS Navywar on terrordeterrence
So what: Cheap asymmetric drone and missile capability is now the killer of expensive naval assets — structurally bearish capital-ship-centric procurement, bullish unmanned systems.
“Neither Iran or Ukraine have a navy and they both control waterways near their fronts.” ▶ 9:53
Middle East Realignment After the Iran War 5
Emanuel says Iran's 40-year, $40 billion-plus investment in the proxy 'octopus' has collapsed: Hamas degraded to defeat, Hezbollah cornered with the Lebanese government actively working with Israel, Damascus lost, and Iraq making headway against the militias.
data_point🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highIranHamasHezbollahLebanonSyriaIraqIsrael
So what: A structurally lower Iranian proxy threat lowers the persistent geopolitical floor under oil and regional risk; residual risk shifts to direct Iranian state action.
“Iran invested 40 years and 40 billion plus in the what Israel described as the octopus, the proxies.” ▶ 19:18
Emanuel argues a non-Assad Syria negotiating a security agreement with Israel would mean all three Six-Day War adversaries — Jordan, Egypt and Syria — have reached resolution with Israel, a strategically different Middle East than the one investors have modeled for 50 years.
thesis🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highSyriaIsraelJordanEgyptSix-Day War
So what: Normalization momentum is the bull case for regional infrastructure and cross-border energy projects; a failed Syria track is the main reversal risk.
Emanuel flags Syria as a potential outlet for a pipeline running from Basra to a Syrian port, routing energy around the Strait of Hormuz.
prediction🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: mediumSyriaBasraIraqStrait of Hormuzoil pipeline

Emanuel splits the region into two camps: the UAE, Bahrain and Kuwait staying firmly with the US, versus Saudi Arabia, Egypt, Pakistan, Turkey and Qatar visibly hedging because two wars have shaken their confidence in American leadership.
data_point🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: mediumUAEBahrainKuwaitSaudi ArabiaEgyptPakistanTurkeyQatar
So what: Hedging states are the ones most likely to accept Chinese technology, currency and security arrangements — a watchlist for where Beijing gains Gulf footholds.
“Saudi Arabia, Egypt, Pakistan, Turkey, and Qatar look like they're hedging.” ▶ 18:41
Emanuel says the India-Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor is the mechanism pulling Gulf states economically and politically closer to the US, and that major Gulf players belong inside the anti-China economic bloc alongside semiconductor supply chains.

Europe's Self-Inflicted Decline 6
Emanuel says Germany is currently losing an estimated 5,000-10,000 manufacturing jobs a month to Chinese competitive pressure, and that the US is squandering the opportunity to convert that pain into a joint transatlantic front against China.
data_point🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: mediumGermanyChinamanufacturingEuropean Union
So what: Quantifies German industrial erosion in real time — a live negative for German industrials and a rising probability of EU trade defense measures against China.

Emanuel dismantles the framing that Europe's damaging decisions were leftist by pointing out Angela Merkel came from the conservative CDU and personally made both the open-door decision on a million Syrian refugees and the post-Fukushima nuclear shutdown that deepened German dependence on Russian energy.

Emanuel concedes Europe has made policy decisions not in its own citizens' interest and has participated in its own suffocation, naming Brussels regulation as having suffocated innovation, entrepreneurship and competitiveness — while endorsing the Draghi plan as the correction.

Emanuel argues immigration and economic stagnation are not separable causes of the European and American political backlash — stagnating incomes, wealth concentration and a political system skewed toward that wealth all mix into the same stew that produced parties like the AfD.
framework🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highAfDEuropeimmigrationincome stagnationwealth concentration
So what: Populism modeled as an income problem rather than only a migration problem means it does not recede when borders tighten — a persistent political risk premium in European assets.

Emanuel says roughly two-thirds of Americans now live paycheck to paycheck and that this income reality, not culture-war framing, is what has reshaped both American politics and the cultural landscape.
data_point🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highUnited Stateshousehold incomecost of livingpolitics

Emanuel characterizes Trump's foreign posture as psychological rather than strategic — kissing up to Putin and Xi while punching down at middle powers like Lula, Merz, Meloni, Carney and Sheinbaum — and says allies have stopped buying it.
thesis🗣 Rahm Emanuelconviction: highDonald TrumpVladimir PutinXiLulaMerzMeloniCarneySheinbaum
