Anthony Pompliano 1 episode(s) · 43 insights

Rent Control, Socialism, and the Fight to Save New York City63 min43 insights2026-08-12
▶ Watch on YouTube
Manhattan Institute president Reihan Salam walks Anthony Pompliano through the mechanics of New York City's affordability crisis, arguing that 1961 downzoning plus the 2019 rent law made rent-stabilized multifamily uneconomic (owners are handing back keys), that $43,000-per-pupil schools and a 300,000-person city payroll deliver poor service because the system optimizes for employment, and that New York State's $268 billion budget has turned Medicaid into the region's real economic base. He then maps how the DSA — revived by Occupy and Bernie Sanders and now staffing Zohran Mamdani's City Hall — built a durable coalition on a deliberately ambiguous socialist brand, and warns that AI and Bitcoin anxiety is being repurposed as a vehicle for UBI and expropriation agendas.
NYC housing supply and the 2019 rent law · 10Cost-per-pupil versus outcomes in NYC schools · 7Public safety staffing and the morale drain · 6Medicaid as New York's real economic base · 5DSA machinery and the socialist brand · 8AI, Bitcoin, and the narrative war for the rising generation · 7
NYC housing supply and the 2019 rent law 10
New York City's 1961 rezoning replaced a regime where six-story apartment buildings could be built almost anywhere with restrictive zoning designed to compete with the suburbs, and the city never course-corrected when it began growing again in the 1980s and 1990s — leaving a permanent structural under-supply of housing.
causal🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highNew York Cityzoninghousingresidential real estate
So what: The supply constraint is statutory and 60+ years old, so NYC rents stay structurally high regardless of who is mayor unless zoning itself is changed.
The 2019 New York rent law is the specific break point that turned expensive housing into an 'extremely intense emergency': it ended the 1990s vacancy-decontrol compromise that let owners raise rent enough to renovate a vacant unit, and made eviction of problematic tenants much harder.
causal🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: high2019 rent lawrent stabilizationvacancy decontrolmultifamilyNew York City
So what: Dates the impairment of NYC rent-stabilized multifamily to a single 2019 statute — a repeal or softening would be the catalyst to re-rate the asset class; no repeal means continued value erosion.
“If a unit becomes vacant, you are not allowed to raise the rent enough to make it worth your while to fix it up.” ▶ 4:58
Decades of rent control degraded NYC multifamily values so severely that some owners burned down their own buildings for insurance settlements, and the city ended up seizing buildings that could not pay property taxes — a precedent Salam invokes as the template for what the current cycle produces.
anecdote🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highrent controlmultifamilyNew York Cityinsurance
So what: Historical base case for how a rent-controlled multifamily cycle ends: distress, abandonment, municipal seizure — the tail risk on NYC stabilized paper is total, not partial.
“if you want to destroy a city, you know, the only thing more effective than carpet bombing is rent control” ▶ 0:00
A multifamily investor-operator active on X reports that his peers are already handing back the keys on building after building in New York City, indicating lender takebacks are happening now rather than as a forecast.
data_point🗣 Anthony Pompliano (host)conviction: mediummultifamilyNew York Citycommercial real estate lending
So what: Watch NYC rent-stabilized loan books at regional lenders and CMBS pools for accelerating handbacks — the distress is contemporaneous, not prospective.
“my friends are handing back the keys on building after building after building” ▶ 6:16
The oft-cited 11-12% return on rent-stabilized NYC buildings is exaggerated because it blends buildings that mix stabilized and market-rate units with buildings that are entirely stabilized; the fully-stabilized ones are 'barely keeping your head above water.'
framework🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highrent stabilizationmultifamilyNew York City
So what: Underwrite NYC multifamily by stabilized-unit percentage, not portfolio averages — blended figures hide which buildings are already uneconomic.
Even at a genuine 11-12% return, running rent-stabilized buildings is unattractive because the operational burden (trash, vermin, painting, problem tenants) is far heavier than other ways to earn the same return — so capital simply exits rather than fights.
thesis🗣 Anthony Pompliano (host)conviction: highmultifamilyrent stabilizationcapital allocation
So what: Risk-adjusted, NYC stabilized housing loses the competition for private capital; expect persistent under-investment and deferred maintenance regardless of headline cap rates.
“There's a lot way, you know, much easier ways to make 11 or 12%.” ▶ 11:14
The activist goal in NYC housing is explicitly 'decommodification' — moving buildings out of private hands into government or nonprofit ownership — but the operating economics do not change under nonprofit ownership, so the gap must be closed by taxpayer subsidy.
thesis🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highdecommodificationsocial housingNYCHApublic housingNew York City
So what: Any social-housing buildout in NYC is a future municipal budget liability, which is a credit consideration for NYC and NY State municipal bonds.
“They literally use the language of decommodification.” ▶ 8:12
The city's own record as landlord argues against public ownership: hundreds of thousands of New Yorkers live in public housing that is substandard and even dangerous, whereas handing seized buildings back to private landlords — often immigrants building wealth — was a documented success story.
contrarian🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highNYCHApublic housingNew York Cityprivate landlords
So what: The empirical case for privatization of distressed housing stock; if NYC re-municipalizes buildings, expect the NYCHA outcome rather than the privatized outcome.
The landlord was chosen as the 'perfect enemy' for a winnable class war because in New York City even someone earning half a million dollars rents and resents their landlord — a coalition-building mechanic, not an economic argument.
framework🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highZohran MamdaniCea WeaverDSAlandlordsNew York City
So what: Explains why anti-landlord policy has unusually broad electoral support in NYC and why it is unlikely to reverse on affordability evidence alone.
New York City systematically blocked Walmart and big-box retail from entering in the 1990s despite available strip-mall and industrial-zoned sites in the outer boroughs, and the requirement to be 'connected' to politicians and organized labor functions as an extra tax on any retailer entering the city.
causal🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highWalmartbig-box retailorganized laborNew York City
So what: A structural explanation for NYC's retail cost premium — and a reason national retailers' NYC unit economics differ from the rest of their footprint.
“There was a systematic effort to prevent Walmart from coming to the city.” ▶ 2:22
Cost-per-pupil versus outcomes in NYC schools 7
New York City spends roughly $43,000 per pupil in its public schools yet delivers lackluster educational performance, which Salam argues is not intrinsic because student achievement improved steadily under Mayor Bloomberg with fewer resources.
data_point🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highNew York City public schoolsMichael Bloombergeducation spending
So what: Per-pupil spending is not the binding constraint on outcomes — a spending-based political program in NYC should not be expected to move achievement.
“we're spending $43,000 per pupil in the New York City public schools” ▶ 13:03
NYC public schools deliver 120 fewer hours of instruction per year than the national average — against roughly 1,100 hours in a school year, that compounds to more than a full year of lost instruction across K-12, and NYC also trails its own charter schools on instructional hours.
data_point🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highNew York City public schoolscharter schoolsinstructional hours
So what: A hard, measurable input gap that explains outcome gaps better than funding does; charters' hour advantage is a durable performance edge.
“we get 120 hours less than the national average” ▶ 19:08
As families with young children leave New York City, schools with fewer than 150 pupils are kept open with full principal and assistant-principal staffing, because the system's organizing purpose has shifted from delivering a service to sustaining employment.
causal🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highNew York City public schoolsteachers unionsadministrators
So what: Enrollment decline in NYC will not produce cost savings — municipal education spending is sticky downward, a fiscal negative for the city's budget path.
“there's this incredible machine that's built up over time that's a machine to extract” ▶ 15:01
More than 100,000 NYC students are enrolled in charter schools and some NYC charters are the best school systems in the United States, but the political class refuses to grant additional charters to the highest-performing operators — Salam's read is that this protects the unionized workforce's market share.
causal🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highcharter schoolsNew York Cityteachers unionscharter cap
So what: The charter cap is a political constraint on the only NYC education segment that works; a cap lift would be a genuine policy surprise worth tracking.
“We are not going to give those operators that are crushing it. We are not going to give them more charters.” ▶ 16:57
Union step-and-lane pay scales reward seniority and master's degrees rather than performance, pushing peak earnings decades out; since teachers improve most in their first five years and little between years 10 and 20, the structure drives young, hungry teachers out of the profession.
framework🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highteachers unionsstep and lane paycharter schools
So what: A reusable model for why unionized labor markets lose their best young talent — and why competing charter networks can outbid on early-career pay.
Charter growth would be fiscally accretive to New York City because families with a credible school option stay and keep paying income, sales and property taxes — but the system optimizes for protecting the administrator class instead of the tax base.
causal🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highcharter schoolsNew York Citytax baseschool choice
So what: Connects school policy directly to NYC's tax-base retention — the driver of municipal revenue and, downstream, city credit.
Pompliano proposes paying principals 50 cents of every dollar they save against their budget, conditioned on holding test-score performance, as a way to surface waste; Salam extends it to routing the surplus back to families as enrichment vouchers or college-savings contributions.
framework🗣 Anthony Pompliano (host)conviction: exploratoryschool budgetsincentive designschool choice
So what: A concrete incentive-design template for public-sector cost reduction that could be tested at district level anywhere.
Public safety staffing and the morale drain 6
The NYPD is below the headcount it needs, officers are working huge overtime, and Salam argues front-end compensation is inadequate to attract strong candidates — which forces weakened hiring standards and pushes experienced officers toward the exits.
causal🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highNYPDNew York Citypublic safetymunicipal budget
So what: Overtime-dependent staffing is a hidden municipal cost line and a leading indicator of a future public-safety deterioration in NYC.
Suburban departments and police forces in Texas and Florida are actively recruiting experienced NYPD officers with better pay and more respect, creating a one-way talent drain out of New York City.
prediction🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highNYPDTexasFloridalaw enforcement
So what: Watch NYPD attrition and lateral-transfer data as a real-time gauge of NYC's public-safety trajectory — the input that historically drove NYC's real-estate and population cycles.
“if you're an NYPD officer with some experience under your belt, I promise you there are uh police departments in Texas and Florida and all over the country that want you there” ▶ 26:23
Homicides and shootings in New York City are improving, but Salam separates that from a distinct and unresolved 'disorder' problem and doubts the improvement is durable given staffing and morale conditions.
thesis🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: mediumNew York CityNYPDcrime statistics
So what: Headline violent-crime stats can stay good while the conditions that produce the next deterioration accumulate — do not read crime data alone as an all-clear on NYC.
Officers who arrest the same individual 14 times and see him back on the street the next day become demoralized, and the practical effect of a police force that doubts the city has its back is officers who do not rush in when danger appears.
causal🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highNYPDcriminal justice reformrecidivism
So what: The transmission channel from bail/prosecution policy to actual street safety runs through officer discretion, not headcount alone — policy changes show up in behavior before they show up in statistics.
Salam's operating principle for public policy is to make government excellent only at what only government can do — armed forces, law enforcement, and the basic order that lets people raise families and build businesses.
framework🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highpublic policygovernmentManhattan Institute
So what: The filter he applies to every spending question; useful for anticipating Manhattan Institute positions on municipal budgets.
“My core guiding principle is always let's make government excellent at what only government can do.” ▶ 25:16
New York City employs over 300,000 people against a population of roughly 8.5 million, and visible disorder — graffiti, broken windows, accumulating trash — is what converts that headcount into taxpayer resentment because residents cannot see value for money.
data_point🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highNew York Citymunicipal employmentbroken windows theory
So what: A 300k-person payroll is the fixed cost underneath NYC's budget; visible disorder is the political trigger that turns fiscal facts into taxpayer flight.
“New York City employs over 300,000 people.” ▶ 28:41
Medicaid as New York's real economic base 5
New York State spends $268 billion, with Medicaid at the center, and the federal government matches every state dollar — creating a structural incentive to spend more state money in order to draw down more borrowed federal dollars.
data_point🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highMedicaidNew York Statefederal matching fundsstate budget
So what: Any tightening of the federal Medicaid match is an outsized shock to New York State's budget and to the healthcare employment it underwrites.
“New York State, you may have heard, spends $268 billion” ▶ 36:25
The CDPAP consumer-directed home health aide program lets recipients hire their own daughter, son, or cousin as the paid caregiver with no meaningful verification of service delivery, and home health aide is simultaneously one of the fastest-growing jobs in New York City.
causal🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highCDPAPMedicaidhome health aidesNew York City
So what: Fast job growth funded by an unverifiable benefit program is not real economic growth — discount NYC health-services employment gains accordingly.
Upstate New York's economy, once driven by manufacturing and tourism, is now anchored by state universities, state prisons and state hospital systems, with hospitals spending more money while serving fewer patients.
data_point🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highupstate New YorkMedicaidstate hospitalspublic sector employment
So what: Upstate NY is effectively a government-transfer economy; regional banks and real estate there are exposed to state fiscal policy rather than private demand.
“Now, you've got a lot of places in upstate New York that are supported by a state university or a state prison or a state hospital system where those hospitals are spending more and more money even though they have fewer and fewer patients.” ▶ 37:47
Because so many nominally private-sector jobs in New York are funded by Medicaid dollars, the state has become what Salam calls an extraction environment, and job creators and investors read it as inhospitable and go elsewhere.
thesis🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highNew York StateMedicaidbusiness formationcapital flight
So what: Frames NY's business-climate problem as circular subsidy dependence rather than tax rates alone — a slower-moving but harder-to-reverse headwind.
New York has moved from merely permitting benefit collection to actively funding community organizers who knock on doors to enroll people, deliberately de-stigmatizing benefits — which Salam argues produces an entitlement mindset that changes immigrant assimilation outcomes.
causal🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highwelfare statebenefits enrollmentimmigrationNew York City
So what: Outreach-driven enrollment mechanically expands program caseloads and costs independent of underlying need — a budget dynamic that compounds.
“we don't want to stigmatize, that's the word, we don't want to stigmatize collecting benefits” ▶ 34:06
DSA machinery and the socialist brand 8
The DSA was founded in 1982 as a near-dead talking shop of anti-communist socialists, many of them passionate supporters of Israel, and was revived in the 2010s by Occupy Wall Street and the Bernie Sanders campaigns — meaning today's organization has almost nothing in common with its founding cohort.
data_point🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highDSAOccupy Wall StreetBernie Sanderssocialism
So what: The DSA's radicalism is a 2010s phenomenon layered onto an old shell; its trajectory is set by the millennial cohort, not institutional history.
The DSA's core appeal to educated millennials was community rather than ideology — people who were not attending a synagogue, mosque, or church found a tight-knit group with a shared vision and a shared enemy.
thesis🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highDSAmillennialssocialism
So what: Movements built on belonging are far stickier than movements built on policy; do not expect the DSA to dissolve when specific policies fail.
Only about 20% of children raised at the 90th income percentile end up at or above that percentile themselves, meaning roughly 80% of kids from affluent families experience downward mobility — the reservoir of frustration Salam says radical politics recruits from.
data_point🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highincome mobilityDSAmillennials
So what: A structural, demographic explanation for elite-educated radicalism that predicts the supply of recruits persists regardless of the economic cycle.
“If your family was the 90th percentile, there's only a 20% chance you're at the 91st or 95th or 99th percentile yourself.” ▶ 41:27
Socialism's power as a brand is precisely its ambiguity — it can mean clean Danish subways or the Bolivarian revolution in Venezuela — and Salam argues the DSA has successfully and deliberately blurred the two to widen its coalition.
framework🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highDSAsocialismDenmarkVenezuela
So what: Polling support for 'socialism' overstates support for its actual program; the gap is where political risk gets mispriced.
“Now the beauty of socialism as a brand right now is actually that socialism can mean anything.” ▶ 46:42
Mainstream Democratic members of Congress in New York already back Medicare for All variants and sit far to the left of Bill Clinton, so what now separates the DSA from the party mainstream is largely branding, aggression, and transgressiveness rather than policy substance.
contrarian🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highDemocratic PartyDSAMedicare for AllBill Clinton
So what: Treating DSA-aligned candidates as a fringe underestimates the policy overlap already present in the mainstream party — the tail risk to markets is broader than the DSA label suggests.
The DSA cadre entering New York politics is disciplined, organized, highly capable and highly radical, and it is running up against a political establishment of time-servers that is not prepared for opponents like that.
thesis🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highDSANew York CityZohran Mamdani
So what: Implies the socialist bloc in NYC governance is durable and likely to keep winning institutional fights — position for policy continuity, not reversion.
“These are people who are disciplined, organized, highly capable, and highly radical.” ▶ 43:51
The composition of New York City's newcomers flipped from roughly two-thirds immigrants and one-third college-educated professionals to the reverse, and while the white share of the population is roughly unchanged since 1990 at 30-35%, it is now transplants with no memory of pre-Giuliani New York rather than multi-generational Irish, Italian and Jewish outer-borough residents.
data_point🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highNew York CitydemographicsRudy Giulianigentrification
So what: The demographic mechanism behind NYC's political shift: an electorate with no lived memory of urban decline will not price the risk of policies that caused it.
The 2019 rent law was driven by people around current mayor Zohran Mamdani — including close adviser Cea Weaver, a leading tenant organizer — who did not live through the Bronx burning and the physical deterioration of NYC buildings.
causal🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highZohran MamdaniCea Weaver2019 rent lawNew York City
So what: Personnel continuity from the 2019 rent law into City Hall signals further housing-policy tightening rather than moderation.
AI, Bitcoin, and the narrative war for the rising generation 7
The shape of AI and Bitcoin is still being discerned, and Salam warns that people with pre-existing agendas — unconditional basic income, dramatic welfare-state expansion, expropriation of private property — are using anxiety and dread about these technologies as a vehicle to advance those agendas.
thesis🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highartificial intelligenceBitcoinuniversal basic incomewelfare state
So what: Expect AI-displacement anxiety to be converted into redistribution policy proposals; that is the political risk channel for tech and crypto holdings, not the technology itself.
“there are a lot of people who already had a pre-cooked pre-existing agenda where they're using this and they're using the anxiety and dread around these new technologies as a vehicle to drive forward their pre-existing agenda” ▶ 50:30
Salam explicitly reframes the fight over AI, crypto and capitalism as not right-versus-left but as competing narratives for winning the rising generation — and says the pro-building narrative currently has no one telling it.
framework🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highartificial intelligenceBitcoinmillennialsGen Z
So what: The contest is narrative distribution, not legislation; media and creator platforms are where the pro-capital coalition is won or lost.
“I don't see these as right versus left battles. I see these as narratives for winning the rising generation.” ▶ 51:13
Salam's 'the real hero is Mark Zuckerberg's dad' framing argues the scarce input in America is not founders but the patient wealth-builders one generation back — dentists who make it possible for a child to attend a great school and own a computer.
framework🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highMark Zuckerbergwealth buildingfamily formation
So what: A reframing of household wealth accumulation as intergenerational infrastructure rather than consumption — the case for long-duration, boring compounding.
“the real hero is not Mark Zuckerberg, it's Mark Zuckerberg's dad” ▶ 51:50
Salam identifies resentment and envy as genuinely powerful motivators that political movements can monetize, but argues the compounding effect of patient reps and building beats them over time — and notes there is currently a lucrative business in selling people the victim message.
thesis🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highsocialismresentmentcapitalism
So what: A read on the attention economy: grievance content is a profitable business model, which is why it scales faster than build-oriented content.
“Envy is a powerful drug. But an even more powerful drug is that thing that you get from getting reps and building something patiently over time.” ▶ 57:49
Salam connects the current wave of anti-Israel radicalism to a broader resentment of visible wealth, power and achievement — arguing the fixation on a foreign policy issue is a symptom of people who feel bereft and unattached to anything larger than themselves.
thesis🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: mediumanti-semitismIsraelDSANew York City
So what: Frames the movement's foreign-policy fixation as downstream of domestic economic alienation — meaning it moves with domestic conditions, not events abroad.
Assimilation is a two-way street in which immigrants adopt local norms and natives partly adopt immigrant norms — so norm enforcement matters: an immigrant arriving when fare evasion is punished assimilates to paying, and one arriving when everyone jumps the turnstile assimilates to that instead.
framework🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highimmigrationassimilationMTAfare evasionbroken windows theory
So what: A concrete mechanism linking visible enforcement of small infractions to long-run social outcomes — the operational case for broken-windows policing.
“if you just see everyone else paying the fair when they get on the subway, you assimilate to that norm” ▶ 33:16
Immigration, not gentrification, was the main driver of New York City's neighborhood revival through the 1980s, 1990s and 2000s: poor immigrants moved into neighborhoods abandoned by middle-class and working-class flight and their economic activity made those neighborhoods safer and better for residents already there.
contrarian🗣 Reihan Salamconviction: highimmigrationgentrificationNew York Cityurban revival
So what: Reframes the gentrification debate with a mechanism that argues for immigration-driven neighborhood investment as an asset-value tailwind, distinct from the current illegal-immigration disorder complaint.

a16z Podcast 1 episode(s) · 42 insights

The New Rules for Founders | Garry Tan on a16z51 min42 insights2026-08-12
▶ Watch on YouTube
YC president Garry Tan argues the per-seat SaaS model and its 10-20x forward-revenue multiple are already broken, that the new unit of labor is a markdown skill file run on a cron (enabling ~$15M ARR in four months with two or three people), and that founders should spend $50-100k/year token-maxing on harnesses like Open Claw to 'live in 2028'. His counter-consensus twist: bureaucracy and human working-memory limits will stretch this transition over ~20 years, so incumbents like Microsoft keep their moats - which he calls the white pill.
Per-seat SaaS regime change · 4Skillification: markdown files as headcount · 6Token maxing and the coming harness wars · 8Institutional slowness as the white pill · 6Agentic management and erasing the API line · 6Founder selection: late bloomers and the 35-45 cohort · 6SF civic turnaround as an exportable playbook · 6
Per-seat SaaS regime change 4
Garry Tan says a pure per-seat SaaS business is 'not totally clear it will exist in another 5 or 10 years' and is now only acceptable as a wedge toward a data or network-effect moat.
thesis🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highSaaSYCAI agents
So what: Directly challenges the terminal-value assumption under most seat-based software comps; favors positioning toward outcome-priced or data-moat businesses.
“a pure like per seat SaaS thing, like not totally clear it will exist in another 5 or 10 years. Like it's perfectly fine as a wedge, but if you're doing that now in 2026, like you better hope that that's like a leap to some sort of moat” ▶ 15:44
The 10-20x next-twelve-months revenue multiple that was treated as an 'iron law' for SaaS two years ago has already stopped being true, according to Tan.
data_point🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highSaaSsoftware multiples
So what: A named-and-dated repricing of the standard software valuation heuristic from the president of YC; watch forward-revenue multiple compression in seat-based software comps.
“SaaS was God, you know, like the 10 20X like next 12 months uh revenue multiple was like, you know, iron law. And like it's hard to believe it was like, oh yeah, no, that's not true at all now.” ▶ 16:06
Tan cites companies going from zero to roughly $15M ARR in about four months with two or three people plus a few hundred agent skill files, implying revenue-per-employee ratios that break conventional startup benchmarks.
data_point🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highYCAI agentsARR
So what: If replicable, it collapses the capital and headcount required per revenue dollar and undermines burn-multiple and headcount-based diligence norms.
“you can go from zero to 15 mil ARR in about 4 months with like two or three people and like hundreds of agent like, you know, a few hundred skill files.” ▶ 26:11
Tan argues the historic YC requirement for co-founders is weakening because vibe coding and agentic coding let one person be a multiple of themselves, though he still believes co-founders are net positive all else equal.
thesis🗣 Garry Tanconviction: mediumYCvibe codingagentic coding
So what: Signals that a top accelerator's screening criteria are shifting toward solo founders, widening the fundable founder pool.
“for the longest time we really really believed that you needed co-founders and I think that everything else being equal, having co-founders is net really really good.”
Skillification: markdown files as headcount 6
Tan's operating model is that any business process should be done once perfectly and then converted into a markdown file plus code plus tests that can be re-run on a cron, making 'a markdown file an employee'.
framework🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highAI agentsmarkdowncronskill files
So what: A concrete, copyable template for where agentic labor substitution actually lands inside a company's cost structure.
“a markdown file is an employee.” ▶ 24:08
Tan says the same skillification loop applies across sales, marketing, customer support and every other function, not just engineering.
thesis🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highsalesmarketingcustomer supportAI agents
So what: Widens the addressable displacement well beyond dev tools into every SG&A line item.
The failure mode of agent-built systems is not intelligence but data hygiene: once you accumulate many markdown/skill files you need provenance, conflict resolution favoring the more recent fact, and a sweeping cron job for accuracy.
causal🗣 Garry Tanconviction: mediumAI agentsprovenanceRAG
So what: Points to memory/provenance/context-governance tooling as the bottleneck layer worth funding rather than raw model capability.
“that's where like provenance matters. That's where um conflict management sort of you know when you have two facts if you have provenance then you're like well this is the more recent one like this is the one that wins.” ▶ 26:51
Building agentic systems reduces to iteratively finding the current bottleneck and instructing the agent to build the software or markdown file that removes it; Tan found himself automated down to only doing black-box verification.
framework🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highG StackQAAI agents
So what: Describes the practical ceiling of current agent autonomy - human residual is verification - which is where the next tooling gap sits.
Tan built G Stack, G Brain and Garyslist.org himself, distilling recorded YC office hours into a markdown script for raising founder ambition that he had to ask Claude to weaken by 90% before open-sourcing.
anecdote🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highG StackG BrainGaryslist.orgClaudeYC
So what: Shows senior investors now build their own agent tooling; also a rare look at YC's advising IP being productized.
“I actually had to go into Claude, and I said, "Reduce this by 90%. Reduce the strength and intensity of this prompt by 90%."” ▶ 17:56
Tan embedded a 'please apply to YC' message inside his open-source agent tooling that only surfaces to top-decile Claude Code users judged by product and design thoughtfulness, using open source as an agentic deal-sourcing funnel.
anecdote🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highYCClaude CodeG StackG Brain
So what: A novel top-of-funnel mechanic - open-source instrumentation as founder-scouting - that other funds will likely copy.
“But only if you're a top 10% user of Claude code.” ▶ 18:27
Token maxing and the coming harness wars 8
Tan claims the consumer frontier products (ChatGPT, Claude) are deliberately compute-constrained, so getting full-strength intelligence requires self-hosted harnesses like Open Claw or Hermes Agent tuned to load 800k-1M tokens per request.
thesis🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highOpenAIChatGPTAnthropicClaudeOpen ClawHermes Agent
So what: Implies a durable gap between shipped consumer capability and raw model capability - and a market for harness/context layers on top of the labs.
“the frontier model labs that are, you you know, letting you use ChatGPT or Claw, I think that they're still pretty constrained on and pretty constrained on, um, amount of compute they want to give you.”
Tan estimates it costs roughly $50,000-$100,000 per year to run agents at full strength on every request, and argues that for a CEO or founder this is worth it because it lets you 'live in 2028' today.
data_point🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highAI agentsOpen Clawcompute costs
So what: Quantifies the price of a capability arbitrage window; also a rough floor on what premium agentic seats could eventually be priced at.
“it costs, I don't know, 50 or 100,000 dollars a year to like use the agents at full strength, like full 150 IQ on every request.” ▶ 23:07
Tan predicts 2027 will be 'the harness wars', with a fight for a billion consumers that only starts once today's frontier compute drops to roughly $50-100, effectively restarting the browser wars.
prediction🗣 Garry Tanconviction: mediumAI harnessesbrowser warsconsumer AI
So what: Dates the consumer AI land-grab to 2027-2029 and ties its trigger to a specific cost threshold investors can track.
“My guess is like next year, 2027, will be like the harness wars.” ▶ 42:53
Tan expects today's frontier pure-model compute to cost about 50 or 100 dollars in two or three years, making cost - not capability - the current gate on consumer AI.
prediction🗣 Garry Tanconviction: mediumcompute costsconsumer AI
So what: A concrete cost-decline curve to test; the crossing point is when free-to-try consumer AI products become viable.
“it's really a cost thing. Like, it's just too expensive right now,” ▶ 42:58
The a16z host frames a barbell in token economics: price per token of the frontier trends toward infinity while the price per token of last week's frontier model collapses to zero, which is what will unlock free-to-try consumer AI.
framework🗣 hostconviction: highAI modelstoken pricingconsumer AI
So what: A clean mental model for why consumer AI margins improve on a lag and why fast-follower model tiers, not frontier, will carry consumer distribution.
“the price per token of frontier is going to infinity. Like, my thesis is infinity dollars a token, you can't have it. And the price per token of the model that was frontier a week ago is collapsing to zero.” ▶ 43:24
The host argues consumer AI is dramatically underbuilt relative to the primitives available, calling it a tragedy that no one has shipped ambitious consumer products despite computers being emotive for the first time in 40-50 years.
thesis🗣 hostconviction: highconsumer AIconsumer software
So what: Flags consumer AI as the underinvested category relative to enterprise/agentic tooling - a stated a16z appetite.
“We have these magical primitives. Like, we have computers that are emotive and have emotional texture for the first time in 40 or 50 years. What a tragedy that we haven't built more ambitious consumer products yet.” ▶ 43:53
Tan expects the next computer to be voice-first with deep memory, built on computer use, diarization and ingestion, converging on a benevolent assistant that knows a user's hopes, fears and desires.
prediction🗣 Garry Tanconviction: mediumvoice interfacesmemorycomputer useconsumer AI
So what: Names the specific capability stack (computer use, memory, diarization, ingestion) where the consumer assistant race will be won.
“it's pretty clearly going to be voice.” ▶ 41:54
Tan publicly revises his own timeline: converting to Open Claw convinced him agentic transformation would happen immediately, and he now believes that view was 'not correct at all'.
contrarian🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highOpen ClawAI adoption
So what: A prominent AI bull explicitly extending his adoption timeline - relevant to anyone underwriting near-term agentic revenue.
“I was convinced that like being becoming a convert to open claw made me very very convinced that it was going to happen right away and then at this point I'm like that's not correct at all.” ▶ 44:09
Institutional slowness as the white pill 6
Tan's counter-consensus take is that bureaucracy, middle management and human cognitive limits will slow AI diffusion so much that the transition takes about 20 years - and he frames that slowness as good news, not a problem.
contrarian🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highAI adoptionmiddle managementlabor markets
So what: Argues against both the doom case and the fast-disruption case; supports incumbents' terminal values and lengthens the runway for displacement trades.
“society is way slower than you think. Government is way slower than you think. Every company in the world is way slower than you think.” ▶ 41:06
Tan says explicitly that startups will not replace incumbents like Microsoft because there are real structural moats, calling this antithetical to what most of Silicon Valley says.
contrarian🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highMicrosoftMag 7incumbents
So what: The head of YC arguing incumbent moats hold is a notable data point against the 'AI kills the incumbents' short thesis.
“there are real motes to these things. Like there are structural reasons why a Microsoft isn't going anywhere.” ▶ 41:14
Tan's core diagnostic for why institutions are unready is that the entire world - business, government, products - is designed around humans who can only hold seven plus or minus two things in their head, while a human plus an agent can hold the equivalent of three Harry Potter books.
framework🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highAI agentsgovernmentmanagement
So what: A reusable screen for which processes get disrupted first: anything whose design constraint was human working memory.
“the world is basically built on things that are built around human beings and human beings can only keep seven plus or minus two things in their head at any given time.” ▶ 30:58
Tan notes YC's Startup School drew 7,000 attendees, mostly 18-22 year olds who became adults during ChatGPT, and argues society will run on their expectations in roughly 20 years - the mechanism behind his slow-diffusion timeline.
causal🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highYCStartup SchoolChatGPTSan Francisco
So what: Generational turnover, not model capability, is his rate-limiter for AI adoption - a testable framing for adoption forecasts.
“we had 7,000 people. Most of them were 18 to 22 years old.” ▶ 40:21
Tan and the host frame the last century of managerial science as possibly a 100-year detour from founder mode, with the implication that agents restore direct founder control and cut intentional work to one to three days a week.
thesis🗣 hostconviction: mediummanagementfounder modeproductivity
So what: If management layers are the compressible cost, the displacement lands on middle management rather than individual contributors.
“maybe that was a 100-year distraction from what was founder mode” ▶ 33:32
Tan's Microsoft anecdote - carrying a baseball bat to the Windows team to get a P3 bug fixed across seven org levels - is his canonical example that big-company coordination failure, not talent, is the waste, and that a startup can and must organize differently.
anecdote🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highMicrosoftWindows Mobilestartups
So what: The mechanism behind his incumbent-inertia thesis: coordination cost is the incumbent tax startups arbitrage.
“An org like Microsoft can't. [music] Yes, but a startup can. Yes, and every startup must.” ▶ 0:14
Agentic management and erasing the API line 6
Brex's Pedro runs agents over meeting transcripts from all his direct reports to see two levels down, detect what is broken and who is in conflict, and walk into meetings he was never in with perfect three-week context.
anecdote🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highBrexOpen ClawAI agentsmanagement
So what: A live enterprise use case for agentic org observability - a category with obvious demand from CEOs and obvious HR/compliance friction.
“he can use uh meetings that he's not in now to figure out like what's broken, what's in conflict, like who's who's in conflict with who.” ▶ 29:39
Brex solved the safety objection to running Open Claw in a highly regulated fintech by open-sourcing a supervisory layer called Craptrack - an agent that watches all network traffic and actions of the agent.
anecdote🗣 Garry Tanconviction: mediumBrexOpen ClawCraptrackfintechAI safety
So what: Agent-supervision/observability layers are the unlock for regulated-industry agent adoption - a fundable wedge.
Tan identifies management failure - the business becoming too big to fit in one person's head - as a defining reason startups and businesses fail, and argues agents plus memory plus retrieval give a CEO ground-truth 'clairvoyance' rather than filtered opinion.
causal🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highAI agentsmanagementretrieval
So what: Reframes agents as a scaling-limit remover for founder-led companies, which raises the ceiling on how far founder mode can scale.
“the business becomes too big to fit in one person's head.” ▶ 30:15
Tan argues AI erases Venkatesh Rao's 'API line' - the divide between those who command APIs and those who serve them - citing YC company Kolabtree shipping a bug-report endpoint built for agents that replies in real time with a triage verdict and a workaround.
thesis🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highKolabtreeVenkatesh RaoUberAPIsAI agents
So what: Agent-to-agent commercial interfaces are an emerging product surface; also a bullish reframe for labor below the API line.
“I actually think AI erases the API line.” ▶ 37:36
Tan invokes the Toyota Production System - letting the line worker change the line because they hold the most context - as the model for where agents should sit, implying the middle organizing layer rather than the executive or the individual contributor.
framework🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highToyotamanagementAI agents
So what: A specific prediction about which org layer agents absorb first: PM-style coordination work, not direction-setting or execution.
“if you are the person doing the work on the line, you should be allowed to change the line because you are the person with the most amount of contacts” ▶ 38:43
The host argues agents let organizations run conflicting approaches in parallel and unbundle conflict from human emotion, which is why decentralized organizations and economies outperform - every idea gets tried.
framework🗣 hostconviction: mediumAI agentsorganizational design
So what: Suggests a testable operating advantage: agent-run parallel exploration as a replacement for consensus-driven roadmapping.
Founder selection: late bloomers and the 35-45 cohort 6
Tan identifies a mega-trend of 35-45 year old repeat founders who know what to build, citing Peter Steinberger, and argues agents let one such person outperform an entire department at a Mag 7 company.
thesis🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highYCPeter SteinbergerMag 7AI agents
So what: Contradicts the young-founder prior that dominates seed investing; experienced-operator-plus-agents may be the highest-alpha founder profile.

Tan says agency and taste are now 'off the charts important' and are practiced rather than innate, positioning his own career of corrected mistakes as evidence against the fixed-high-agency-population view the host raises.
thesis🗣 Garry Tanconviction: mediumtalentagencytaste
So what: Bears on whether AI tooling broadens the founder funnel or just amplifies the same top 20% - the host's explicit challenge.
“suddenly agency and taste like off the charts important now” ▶ 19:47
Tan's repeated career error - leaving web programming in 2003 because the web was declared dead, then turning down Peter Thiel's offer to co-found Palantir for a Microsoft level-60 promotion - is his evidence that following consensus over direct knowledge is the dominant destroyer of returns.
anecdote🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highPalantirPeter ThielJoe LonsdaleMicrosoftFacebook
So what: The canonical opportunity-cost story behind YC's 'don't LARP' doctrine; useful lens on why consensus-chasing kills option value.

Tan says 'what's hot and what should I work on' is the wrong question and the right one is what you uniquely know - the operating principle behind his 'don't LARP' meme at Startup School.
framework🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highYCStartup School
So what: A selection heuristic worth applying to founder pitches: unique first-party knowledge over thematic fit.
“that's the wrong question to ask. Like the right question is like, "What are you interested in? What do you know uniquely?"” ▶ 4:17
Tan's pattern-match for a great opportunity is that it looks like a toy on the fringe of the idea maze and you cannot find the intellectual edge of the conversation about it.
framework🗣 Garry Tanconviction: highChris Dixonidea mazeHomebrew Computer Club
So what: A concrete screen for early-stage sourcing: unfindable intellectual edge as a positive signal.
“a good measure of how interesting the thing is is can you find the edge?” ▶ 6:06
Tan says everything that has worked in his life is 'kind of a cult' - a belief that flies in the face of orthodoxy - and that first-party investigation produces knowledge that published sources do not.

SF civic turnaround as an exportable playbook 6

Tan argues fixing local government mechanically fixes state and national over a five to ten year horizon because winners rise through a functional system, and that human coordination - not intelligence - is the binding constraint on civic problems.

The Growth Strategy Trapping The Fed | Darius Dale46 min38 insights2026-08-12
▶ Watch on YouTube
Darius Dale of 42 Macro argues the US is locked in 'paradigm C' — deliberately running the economy hot to grow out of ~100% debt-to-GDP — which is simultaneously the best policy option and the one that guarantees stress in the long end, with his model putting 10-year Treasury fair value at 5.8%. He reads markets as a risk-on reflation regime (71% signal strength) with rising r-star (1.47-1.78% versus a 1.21% real fed funds rate) explaining unusually wide factor dispersion, and expects a not-yet-priced dovish surprise within three to six months as Kevin Warsh's five task forces report.

Paradigm C: Growing Out of the Debt 6
42 Macro frames US sovereign debt policy as a menu of options — cut (paradigm B), grow (paradigm C), print (paradigm D) — and says the US has been running paradigm C (running the economy hot) since April 2025 with a sprinkling of paradigm D since the Fed launched its reserve management purchase program in December.
framework🗣 Darius Daleconviction: high42 MacroFederal ReserveUS Treasuryreserve management purchase program
So what: Positions the regime as deliberately reflationary policy, arguing for long risk assets and nominal-GDP-linked exposure over duration.
“we have paradigm C, which is running the economy hot, with the sprinkling of paradigm D, which is printing” ▶ 3:11
The core trap is that growing out of the debt is the best available option yet it is the same option that breaks the bond market, because a hot nominal economy makes investors prefer stocks, corporate credit and spread products to Treasuries.
causal🗣 Darius Daleconviction: highUS Treasury marketcorporate creditequitiesnominal GDP
So what: Implies structurally higher long yields and a persistent bid for risk over duration for as long as the grow strategy holds.
“the grow your way out of the solution is the best option but ultimately the best option is the same exact option that causes problems in the bond market” ▶ 32:29
Austerity (paradigm B) is politically off the table because the US already has an inequality problem and AI's labor-market effects are pushing people close to the streets — DOGE's failure last spring is cited as proof.
thesis🗣 Darius Daleconviction: highDOGEElon MuskAIfiscal policy
So what: Rules out a deficit-cutting deflation scare, so fade positioning that requires fiscal consolidation.

Dale frames the policy extremes in terms of political outcomes: excessive austerity ends in war and excessive money printing ends in civil war, so the authorities' only viable path is keeping the economy humming.
framework🗣 Darius Daleconviction: mediumfiscal policymonetary policyinflation

“cutting too much winds up with war, printing too much winds up with civil war” ▶ 32:01
US debt-to-GDP is around 100%, a level last seen right after World War II, but this time it was reached without a war or a recession.
data_point🗣 Darius Daleconviction: highUS debt-to-GDPUS Treasury

“News flash we haven't been in war. We haven't been in recession either.” ▶ 30:56

thesis🗣 Darius Daleconviction: highUS Treasury market42 Macro

Building a $200M Bootstrapped Chess Empire with Chess.com CEO Erik Allebest46 min37 insights2026-08-13
▶ Watch on YouTube
Chess.com CEO Erik Allebest details how a $56K bankruptcy-auction domain became a ~$200M-revenue, 650-person, never-primary-funded business with 10M DAU and 250M+ registered members, whose institutional cap table (General Atlantic, now CVC) was built entirely through secondary purchases. The strategic core: superhuman AI expanded rather than killed chess, anti-cheat data is the real moat in an AI-output world, and the ratings playbook is now being ported to poker via Gambit.

Bootstrapping a market VCs called uninvestable 6
Chess.com was bought as a bankrupt domain name for ~$55-56K in 2005 and grew to ~$200M in annual revenue with zero primary venture capital raised, showing that a 'niche passion' consumer category can compound into a nine-figure business without outside growth capital.
data_point🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comErik Allebestventure capitalStanford GSB
So what: A live counterexample for allocators sizing consumer-community assets: category TAM estimates from institutional investors were wrong by orders of magnitude, so 'small market' screens can hide compounding assets.
“we just really kind of grew at the speed of cash.” ▶ 4:54
Essentially every venture investor Allebest pitched on Sand Hill Road while at Stanford told him online chess was uninvestable and he should get a real job, which is why the company never raised primary capital.
anecdote🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highSand Hill RoadStanfordChess.comventure capital
So what: Consensus VC rejection of a category is weak evidence about the category itself; investors screening consumer 'hobby' markets should discount peer signaling.
“most of them said this is uninvestable. You should get a real job. Don't waste your time with this.” ▶ 4:17
Allebest argues capital intensity, not ambition, should determine whether a company raises: pure software-and-content businesses with no large incumbent bearing down on them can afford the slow, self-funded road, while hardware, inventory, or contested land-grab markets cannot.
framework🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comventure capitalsoftwarehardware
So what: A screen for whether a founder's fundraising plan is structurally justified or cargo-culted: absence of a fast-moving incumbent is the key variable that makes bootstrapping viable.
Chess as a product category has no patent protection and faces thousands to tens of thousands of competing apps and sites, so Allebest attributes durable share entirely to product experience, community, and content rather than any legal or technical moat.
causal🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comLichessapp storesICCFree Internet Chess ServerYahoo Chess
So what: In zero-IP consumer categories, execution quality is the only compounding moat; underwrite retention and product cadence, not defensibility claims.
“chess is like not patentable. There's you know thousands if not tens of thousands of chess apps in the app store” ▶ 8:15
Allebest frames Lichess as the open-source 'Linux' of chess that serves a distinct community segment rather than a competitive threat, arguing the two coexist because Chess.com's revenue funds investments in growing the overall game.
thesis🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: mediumLichessChess.comopen source
So what: A free open-source alternative did not commoditize the paid leader here, a useful precedent when assessing open-source risk to consumer subscription businesses.
Allebest's founder advice is to invert the 2000s startup playbook he was given: he hired a friend from San Jose State rather than a Stanford technical cofounder, worked remotely, raised nothing, spent nothing on customer acquisition, and deliberately picked a tiny market.
contrarian🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comStanfordSan Jose Statecustomer acquisition
So what: Signals that paid-CAC-plus-big-market orthodoxy is optional in games and community categories where organic content and word of mouth carry acquisition.
“hire a technical founder from Stanford, raise a bunch of money, get an office, go after a big market, spend a bunch of money on customer acquisition. And we literally just did like all the opposite of that.” ▶ 35:41
Chess demand: ratcheting step-functions, not a fad 6
Chess.com currently runs about 10 million daily active users, 40-50 million monthly actives, more than 250 million registered members, roughly $200M+ revenue this year, and about 650 employees, all fully remote.
data_point🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.com
So what: Hard baseline numbers for valuing the asset and for benchmarking any competing games or hobby-community platform on DAU-to-revenue conversion (~$20 revenue per DAU per year).
Chess.com's usage grew via discrete non-repeating catalysts that each ratcheted the floor higher rather than mean-reverting: ~1M DAU pre-2020, a COVID plus Queen's Gambit spike peaking in 2022, then a second 2023 spike from short-form content, the Mittens bot, and the cheating scandal, each leaving a permanently higher baseline.
data_point🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comQueen's GambitCOVIDMittens botshort-form video
So what: The pattern to watch in viral consumer assets is whether post-spike troughs are above prior peaks; that ratchet, not peak DAU, is what justifies rerating the business.
“this might just be a flash in the pan like treadmills and sourdough bread” ▶ 0:04
Management did not internally believe chess was a large durable business until 2024, when the post-spike baseline held and kept compounding, meaning the conviction lagged the numbers by roughly four years.
anecdote🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.com
So what: Even insiders systematically underestimate ratchet-driven consumer businesses; outside investors buying during the 'is it a fad' window had a multi-year mispricing to exploit.
“in 2024, we're like, "Oh my gosh, like this thing is actually sticking around. It's actually growing and compounding over time."” ▶ 10:41
Allebest treats the gap between 250 million registered members and 10 million daily actives as the core growth thesis, targeting 50 million DAU and eventually a billion players from reactivation rather than net-new category creation.
thesis🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.com
So what: Growth here is a latent-base reactivation problem, which is cheaper and higher-margin than paid acquisition; monitor DAU/registered ratio as the KPI.
“if 250 million people have said they want to play chess at some point on chess.com, like why don't we have 50 million daily active users?” ▶ 5:55
Chess.com deliberately redefined the identity of a 'chess player' downward from the traditional 2000-rating threshold to include 500-rated beginners, and Allebest credits that narrative change plus mobile and entertaining content for chess's mainstream cultural adoption.
causal🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comLouis VuittonLewis HamiltonElo rating
So what: Expanding who counts as a participant is a repeatable TAM-expansion lever for skill-based products; look for the same move in poker, go, and other rated games.
Rapid growth was operationally destabilizing rather than easy, producing scalability problems across product and tech and what Allebest calls a white-knuckle period, with the business only stabilizing in the last 12 months as the 650-person remote org matured.
anecdote🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comremote work
So what: Growth-driven infrastructure strain is an underappreciated execution risk in consumer platforms riding a viral ratchet.
“what it means to be a 650 person fully remote company” ▶ 11:33
Secondary-only cap table: liquidity without dilution 4
Chess.com's entire institutional cap table has been built through secondary purchases with no primary capital infusion, including General Atlantic's 2020-2021 entry and the recent CVC investment, so the company remains bootstrapped in cash terms while providing shareholder liquidity.
data_point🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comCVCGeneral Atlantic
So what: A template for profitable consumer businesses: secondary-only rounds recycle ownership and set marks without dilution, and create recurring deal flow for buyers as each holder's fund life expires.
“no no no capital needed to to grow this business” ▶ 17:30
Allebest describes a recurring pattern where individual investors promised to hold Chess.com forever and then needed liquidity roughly 3-5 years later, forcing the company up the investor-size ladder from angels to growth equity to large PE.
causal🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comGeneral AtlanticCVCprivate equity
So what: Fund-life mechanics, not company need, drive the ownership churn in profitable private assets; expect repeat secondary processes on a predictable clock.
“I'll hold on to this forever I just want to be a part of this. And then like 5 years later, like I need to get out of this.” ▶ 15:34
Against the common trope, Allebest says private equity ownership made Chess.com operationally better rather than extractive, contributing industry pattern-matching, product and community engagement, and forecasting and operating rigor the founder-run company had lacked.
contrarian🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highCVCGeneral AtlanticChess.comprivate equity
So what: Counter-evidence for the blanket 'PE ruins consumer products' view; the differentiator he cites is a sponsor with gaming, sports, and community domain depth.
“you kind of hear the tropes about private equity and, you know, where they where they give you money and then just say work harder or whatever, do better.” ▶ 17:53
Allebest reports the PE selection process was mostly unpleasant and that most firms he met focused on the wrong things, with fit determined by whether the sponsor believed mission and community focus would produce the financial outcome.
anecdote🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highprivate equityGeneral AtlanticCVCChess.com
So what: For sponsors competing for founder-controlled profitable assets, domain credibility and mission alignment beat price in winning allocation.
What happens to a game after machines beat humans 5
Chess is the longest-running natural experiment in human interest surviving superhuman machines, and 30 years after Deep Blue beat Kasparov humans care about chess far more than they did, falsifying the prediction that solving a domain kills human participation.
thesis🗣 hostconviction: highDeep BlueGarry KasparovchessAI
So what: The base case for AI's effect on human-skill markets is expansion, not extinction, which supports valuations of skill-competition, coaching, and spectator businesses.
Allebest describes a two-phase AI effect on chess quality: brute-force engines like Stockfish made elite play boring by pushing everyone toward grinding perfect endgames, then neural-net self-play engines like Leela Chess Zero beat Stockfish with aggressive, unconventional play and made the game more exciting than ever.
causal🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highStockfishLeela Chess Zeroneural networksself-play reinforcement learningchess
So what: Machine superiority degraded then regenerated the human product; the architecture of the AI, not its strength, determined whether it made the domain dull or vibrant.
“computers at first made chess a little bit more boring, but then made it so much more exciting. And we're seeing more exciting chess than we ever have.” ▶ 21:57
Allebest's explanation for chess's survival is that humans fundamentally want to do human things - compete against other humans, make things, solve problems - so machine dominance redirects rather than destroys demand, and he extends the analogy to AI in mathematics and protein folding.
framework🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highAIchessmathematicsprotein folding
So what: A transferable model for which human activities survive automation: those where the point is the human doing it, not the output.
“fundamentally, humans want to do human stuff.” ▶ 20:24
The host argues that people demonstrably still want to acquire human skills and still appreciate exceptional human performance even when machines outperform, so the claim that human skill becomes irrelevant under superhuman AI is nonsense as a technological matter.
contrarian🗣 hostconviction: highAIAGIhuman skillgames
So what: Supports investing in human-performance, live-competition, and skill-training assets as AI capability rises, while the real risks sit in guardrails and distribution of gains.
“the idea that human skill is no longer relevant in the world of superhuman AI just as a a product of the technology itself is nonsense” ▶ 39:36
Allebest expects that in a more capable and abundant world people will spend substantially more time playing games, framing games as a primary destination for freed human time.
prediction🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: mediumgamesAIabundance
So what: Directly bullish for gaming attention share if AI compresses working hours; a long-dated thesis for games and interactive entertainment exposure.
Skill acquisition has no shortcut, only better tools 4
With visibility into millions of players at every rating level, Allebest says the data shows no shortcut to expertise: improvement comes from repetition of small building blocks like puzzles and mistake review, and the only real leverage is making those reps easier, not skipping them.
framework🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comskill acquisitionpuzzles
So what: Caps the disruption risk to practice-based edtech from AI: tools that reduce friction on reps monetize, tools promising to bypass reps do not work.
“there's really not shortcuts to the process. There's shortcuts in the tools.” ▶ 27:51
Allebest's mental model for compounding is to take any given day and multiply it by a thousand, so five puzzles a day equals 5,000 puzzles and a person's trajectory is set by daily habits rather than intensity.
framework🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highhabitsskill acquisition
So what: A crisp retention frame for consumer subscription products: daily-habit design, not session depth, drives lifetime value.
“if you just take any given day in your life and multiply that by a thousand, that's kind of what your life looks like” ▶ 25:41
Allebest says access to AI and online tools is already producing dramatically faster skill development in young people, citing seven-year-olds giving grandmasters a run and a ten-year-old prodigy, plus his own 18-year-old son who self-taught game development on YouTube and uses AI for research.
data_point🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highAIYouTubechessgrandmasters
So what: Compression of time-to-mastery is observable now in chess ratings data, a leading indicator for what AI tutoring does to other skill hierarchies.
Allebest concedes that research shows people who use AI in certain areas stop thinking and engaging their brains, and that AI tools carry bias, sycophancy, and ego-inflation problems, so guardrails are required for AI-assisted learning to compound rather than atrophy skill.
causal🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: mediumAIeducationLLMs
So what: Product risk for AI-tutoring companies: passive answer delivery may measurably degrade outcomes, so effortful-engagement design is the differentiator schools and regulators will screen for.
Anti-cheat as the integrity layer for AI-era competition 3
Chess.com treats anti-cheating as a major, deliberately undisclosed cost center running statistical and machine-learning models over its proprietary corpus of how humans versus computers actually play, split into two workstreams for casual play and for prize-money professional events.
framework🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comanti-cheatmachine learningcheating scandal
So what: The behavioral dataset for distinguishing human from machine output is the real defensible asset and generalizes to exams, hiring, and any AI-era integrity market.
“we have a whole set of tools, which I'm not going to get into because I don't like to give ideas to people on how we do it” ▶ 30:13
Allebest states cheating cannot be prevented, only detected and punished, because you cannot put a webcam in every home, so the goal is high catch rates plus banning and education rather than elimination.
thesis🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comanti-cheatAI
So what: Sets realistic expectations for the entire AI-detection category: detection-and-deterrence economics, not prevention, is the investable business model.
“cheating it can't be prevented because you can't, you know, you can't put a webcam in every single person's home.” ▶ 31:05
Chess dealt with machine-assisted cheating decades before LLMs and the predicted existential threat to the game never materialized, which Allebest offers as evidence that domains facing superhuman AI output can defend integrity with technology.
contrarian🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highchessAIchess enginesChess.com
So what: A precedent argument against the thesis that AI destroys any field where output can be faked; the arms race resolved in favor of the platform holding the data.
Gambit and porting the ratings playbook to other classic games 4
Chess.com has launched Gambit at gambit.com, a poker product built on the chess playbook of skill ratings rather than chip accumulation, and plans to roll out additional classic games on the same template with a small dedicated team.
data_point🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highGambitgambit.comChess.compoker
So what: An option on Chess.com becoming a multi-game rated-competition platform; watch Gambit adoption as the test of whether the playbook transfers off chess.
“we recently launched Gambit at gambit.com, which is our poker um site” ▶ 34:06
Allebest predicts players will come to care about a poker rating as much as about money because rating is a purer reflection of skill, making rating the retention currency in a category historically monetized through stakes.
prediction🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highGambitpokerrating systemsChess.com
So what: If true, it lets a poker product build engagement without real-money gambling exposure, sidestepping licensing and regulatory drag.
“losing a 100 bucks at a table, it's not my favorite thing, but I don't care that much. But honestly, losing, you know, 100 rating points on my on poker, that just like really bothers me.” ▶ 44:59
Gambit's current poker rating algorithm factors opponents' ratings, chips won, and hands played, and Allebest concedes it is still evolving because stake size materially changes how people play, an unsolved design problem for skill measurement in stochastic games.
framework🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: mediumGambitpokerrating systemsElo
So what: Rating design in luck-heavy games is the technical bottleneck to porting the chess model; solving it would unlock a large category of games for skill-based ranking.
Allebest argues chess wins attention precisely because AI makes content infinite: with 10,000 new board games generatable from a prompt, the value of a fixed-rule, no-loot-box, no-new-skins classic rises as a shared reference point, the same way listeners return to the Beatles amid infinite music.
thesis🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highchessAI-generated gamesmusicgamingthe Beatles
So what: A scarcity-through-abundance thesis: as generative AI floods content markets, legacy fixed-canon IP and rulesets should gain relative attention share and pricing power.
“you could give an AI a prompt and you could have 10,000 new board games tomorrow” ▶ 31:46
AI inside a 650-person consumer company 5
Chess.com's CTO built an internal system called GNS, an authentication and knowledge layer spanning the organization with permissions, ahead of the current industry realization that agents need exactly that substrate, and the company is also building proprietary martech.
data_point🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comGNSmartechAI agents
So what: Confirms enterprise demand for permissioned knowledge and identity layers as the prerequisite for agent deployment, a category where mid-market buyers are currently building in-house.
“we have our own internal system called GNS which is like a like an authentication and knowledge layer that goes across our whole organization” ▶ 41:01
Chess.com's stated goal for agentic development is compressing elapsed time between spotting a problem or opportunity and shipping code to users, explicitly attacking the handoff chain of scoping, roadmapping, building, testing, and QA rather than just coding speed.
thesis🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comagentic developmentsoftware development
So what: The buyer's ROI metric for AI coding tools is cycle time from insight to production, not lines of code, which favors tools spanning the whole SDLC over autocomplete.
“have an idea or see a problem, fix a problem” ▶ 41:28
AI is already deployed at Chess.com in customer support automation alongside human agents, in analyst-facing data querying via LLMs, and in product research, with support described as a symbiosis rather than a headcount replacement.
data_point🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comcustomer supportLLMsdata analytics
So what: A real deployment map for AI in a mid-size consumer company: support, analytics, and dev velocity are where budget actually lands first.
Chess.com is prototyping an on-demand AI chess coach that reviews a user's recent games, delivers feedback and recommendations, supports chat, and benchmarks a player's aggregated stats against peers and players one rung above to identify what to improve next.
prediction🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highChess.comAI coachgame reviewpersonalized learning
So what: A concrete monetizable AI feature in a subscription consumer product; peer-benchmarked improvement paths are the mechanic likely to lift conversion and retention.
“you can have a chess coach in your pocket. Obviously, it's not a replacement for a human.” ▶ 42:49
Allebest believes superhuman AI is coming and accelerating but argues the danger is cultural rather than technological, pointing to too much money already flowing to too few from big tech and expressing low confidence in the billionaires and politicians currently in charge.
thesis🗣 Erik Allebest (Chess.com CEO)conviction: highAGIASIbig techAI policywealth concentration
So what: An operator outside the AI industry voicing distribution-of-gains concern, the sentiment channel that eventually drives regulatory and tax risk for large-cap AI incumbents.
“too much money is flowing into the hands of too few and we're not doing enough as as humans to share what we're creating” ▶ 37:41
